# On the regularity theory for mixed local and nonlocal weighted quasilinear elliptic equations

**arXiv ID**: 2608.02557v1
**Authors**: Sanjit Biswas, Prashanta Garain
**Published**: 2026-08-03
**Categories**: math.AP
**Comments**: 54 pages, comments are welcome
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.02557v1

## Abstract

We investigate a broad class of mixed local and nonlocal degenerate $p$-Laplace equations with general right-hand sides. The degeneracy is governed by Muckenhoupt $A_p$-weights, yielding a highly nonuniform elliptic framework in which both the local and nonlocal operators may degenerate simultaneously. We establish a comprehensive local regularity theory, including local boundedness and lower semicontinuity of weak subsolutions, weak Harnack inequalities for weak supersolutions, Harnack inequalities, and local Hölder continuity of weak solutions. Our approach combines weighted analytic techniques with the De Giorgi--Nash--Moser iteration method, adapted to the mixed local--nonlocal setting. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first systematic regularity theory for mixed local and nonlocal equations with Muckenhoupt weights. In particular, our results are new even for homogeneous linear equations ($p=2$) under the natural assumption $w\in A_2$, and therefore substantially extend the existing regularity theory for mixed local--nonlocal equations to a degenerate weighted framework with general right-hand sides.

## On the regularity theory for mixed local and nonlocal weighted quasilinear elliptic equationsSanjit Biswas111Department of Mathematical Sciences, Indian Institute of Science Education and Research, Berhampur, 760010, Odisha, India, Email: sanjitbiswas410@gmail.comand Prashanta Garain222Department of Mathematical Sciences, Indian Institute of Science Education and Research, Berhampur, 760010, Odisha, India, Email: pgarain92@gmail.com

## Abstract

We investigate a broad class of mixed local and nonlocal degeneratepp-Laplace equations with general right-hand sides. The degeneracy is governed by MuckenhouptApA_{p}-weights, yielding a highly nonuniform elliptic framework in which both the local and nonlocal operators may degenerate simultaneously. We establish a comprehensive local regularity theory, including local boundedness and lower semicontinuity of weak subsolutions, weak Harnack inequalities for weak supersolutions, Harnack inequalities, and local Hölder continuity of weak solutions. Our approach combines weighted analytic techniques with the De Giorgi–Nash–Moser iteration method, adapted to the mixed local–nonlocal setting. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first systematic regularity theory for mixed local and nonlocal equations with Muckenhoupt weights. In particular, our results are new even for homogeneous linear equations (p=2p=2) under the natural assumptionw∈A2w\in A_{2}, and therefore substantially extend the existing regularity theory for mixed local–nonlocal equations to a degenerate weighted framework with general right-hand sides.

Keywords: Mixed local and nonlocal weightedpp-Laplace equations, Muckenhoupt weights, De Giorgi-Nash-Moser theory, local boundedness, Harnack inequality, Hölder continuity, semicontinuity.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 35B65, 35J70, 35J92, 35R11.

## 1Introduction and main results

## 1.1Introduction

The interaction between local and nonlocal diffusion has attracted considerable attention in recent years owing to its numerous applications in nonlinear analysis, probability theory, continuum mechanics, phase transitions, anomalous diffusion, image processing, and material science, see[23,25]and the references therein. Mixed local–nonlocal equations combine the classical diffusion generated by local differential operators with long-range interactions described by nonlocal operators, thereby providing a more realistic mathematical framework for modeling several physical, biological, and engineering phenomena. Although the regularity theory for purely local and purely nonlocal equations has witnessed remarkable developments over the past decades, the corresponding theory for mixed equations remains comparatively less understood. The analysis becomes significantly more challenging in the presence of Muckenhoupt weights, where the interaction between the local and nonlocal operators occurs in a highly nonhomogeneous geometric setting.

The primary objective of the present paper is to develop a comprehensive local regularity theory for a broad class of mixed local and nonlocal weighted quasilinear equations. More precisely, we establish local boundedness of weak subsolutions, lower semicontinuity, weak Harnack inequalities for weak supersolutions, Harnack inequalities, and local Hölder continuity of weak solutions to the mixed local and nonlocal weighted equationℳα​u=ℱ​(x,u)in​Ω,\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}u=\mathcal{F}(x,u)\quad\text{in }\Omega,(1.1)

whereΩ⊂ℝn\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}is a bounded domain withn≥2n\geq 2. The operatorℳα\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}and the nonlinearityℱ\mathcal{F}are explicitly defined below in (1.4) and (1.2), respectively.

Our motivation stems from the fact that, unlike the purely local and purely nonlocal settings, a regularity theory for mixed local and nonlocal weighted equations is essentially absent from the existing literature. To the best of our knowledge, even in the linear casep=2p=2, under the natural assumptionw∈A2w\in A_{2}(the Muckenhoupt weights), and in the homogeneous caseℱ≡0\mathcal{F}\equiv 0, the fundamental qualitative properties of weak solutions, such as local boundedness, weak Harnack inequalities, Harnack inequalities, and local Hölder continuity, have not yet been established. Consequently, the results obtained in the present paper are new even for mixed weighted linear equations and provide, to the best of our knowledge, the first systematic regularity theory for this class of problems.

Unless otherwise mentioned, throughout the rest of the paper, the nonlinearityℱ:Ω×ℝ→ℝ\mathcal{F}:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}is a measurable function assumed to satisfy the natural growth condition|ℱ​(x,ξ)|≤|f​(x)|+|g​(x)|​|ξ|p−1,|\mathcal{F}(x,\xi)|\leq|f(x)|+|g(x)||\xi|^{p-1},(1.2)

for almost every(x,ξ)∈Ω×ℝ(x,\xi)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}, whereffandggare real-valued measurable functions satisfyingfw,gw∈Llocq​(Ω,w),\frac{f}{w},\,\frac{g}{w}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{q}(\Omega,w),

with1<p<∞1<p<\infty,w∈Apw\in A_{p}(the Muckenhoupt weights defined below in section 2), andq>κ′q>\kappa^{\prime}(given below in (2.19)) together with the conditionsupBr​(x0)⋐Ωrp​(⨏Br​(x0)(|g|w)q​w​𝑑x)1q≤M,\displaystyle\sup_{B_{r}(x_{0})\Subset\Omega}r^{p}\left(\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left(\frac{|g|}{w}\right)^{q}w\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq M,(1.3)

for some constantM>0M>0.

## Remark 1.2.

where P.V. denotes the principal value and the kernelK:ℝn×ℝn→ℝK:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}is a symmetric Lebesgue measurable function such that for almost everyx,y∈ℝnx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, we haveλ​w​(x)​w​(y)w​(Bx,y)≤K​(x,y)≤Λ​w​(x)​w​(y)w​(Bx,y),\displaystyle\lambda\frac{w(x)w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})}\leq K(x,y)\leq\Lambda\frac{w(x)w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})},(1.7)

withBx,y:=B|x−y|2​(x+y2)B_{x,y}:=B_{\frac{|x-y|}{2}}(\frac{x+y}{2}),w​(Bx,y):=∫Bx,yw​(z)​𝑑zw(B_{x,y}):=\int_{B_{x,y}}w(z)\;dzandλ≥0,Λ>0\lambda\geq 0,\Lambda>0are constants.

Consequently, equation (1.1) generalizes the mixed anisotropic and fractional weightedpp-Laplace equation−div⁡(w​(x)​H​(∇u)p−1​∇ξH​(∇u))+α​(−Δp,w)s​u=ℱ​(x,u)in​Ω,-\operatorname{div}\!\left(w(x)H(\nabla u)^{p-1}\nabla_{\xi}H(\nabla u)\right)+\alpha(-\Delta_{p,w})^{s}u=\mathcal{F}(x,u)\quad\text{in }\Omega,

which, in turn, generalizes the mixed local and nonlocal weightedpp-Laplace equation−div⁡(w​(x)​|∇u|p−2​∇u)+α​(−Δp,w)s​u=ℱ​(x,u)in​Ω.-\operatorname{div}\!\left(w(x)|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\right)+\alpha(-\Delta_{p,w})^{s}u=\mathcal{F}(x,u)\quad\text{in }\Omega.

Therefore, equation (1.1) encompasses a broad class of local and nonlocal equations arising in nonlinear analysis.

The regularity theory for nonlinear elliptic equations has a long and distinguished history. In the purely local setting, qualitative properties of weak solutions to thepp-Laplace equation, including local boundedness, Harnack inequalities, Hölder continuity, and higher regularity, are by now classical. We refer to[24,44,46,50,52]and the references therein for comprehensive accounts. The weighted theory has also received considerable attention. A pioneering contribution is due to[27], where the authors established the Harnack inequality and Hölder continuity for weak solutions of the linear weighted equationdiv⁡(w​(x)​∇u)=0,\operatorname{div}(w(x)\nabla u)=0,

under the assumptionw∈A2w\in A_{2}. Their work laid the foundation for the study of degenerate elliptic equations with Muckenhoupt weights. Subsequently, nonlinear weighted equations of the formΔp,w​u=0in​Ω,\Delta_{p,w}u=0\quad\text{in }\Omega,

and its associated nonhomogeneous version were investigated in[36,49,53]and the references therein. Very recently, regularity theory for double phase problems with Muckenhoupt condition have been studied in[2,48,51].

The regularity theory for nonlocal equations has undergone remarkable developments over the last two decades. Interior regularity, Harnack inequalities, and Hölder continuity for fractional and integro-differential equations have been established in numerous works; see, for example,[12,15,16,21,22,34,37,38,39]. In contrast, the weighted nonlocal theory is still in its infancy. To the best of our knowledge, the only work establishing regularity results for weighted fractionalpp-Laplace equations is[5]. More precisely, the authors proved local boundedness of weak subsolutions, weak Harnack inequalities for weak supersolutions, Harnack inequalities, and local Hölder continuity of weak solutions to(−Δp,w)s​u=0in​Ω,(-\Delta_{p,w})^{s}u=0\quad\text{in }\Omega,

under the assumptions1<p<∞1<p<\infty,0<s<10<s<1, andw∈Apw\in A_{p}.

Motivated by these developments, the regularity theory for mixed local and nonlocal equations has attracted considerable attention in recent years. Early contributions in the linear setting relied primarily on probabilistic techniques; see, for instance,[4,18,28]. Subsequently, purely analytical methods have been developed to study both linear mixed equations, see[1,7,8,9,25]and the references therein. For nonlinear mixed equations, leading to local boundedness, Hölder continuity, weak Harnack inequalities, and Harnack inequalities among other regularity estimates in the unweighted setting can be found in[3,10,11,13,19,20,29,30,31,32,33]and the references therein. These works have significantly advanced the understanding of mixed diffusion phenomena.

The preceding discussion naturally raises the question of whether the classical regularity theory can be extended to mixed local and nonlocal equations in the weighted setting. The present paper provides an affirmative answer. The interaction between the local and nonlocal weighted operators gives rise to substantial analytical difficulties that cannot be addressed by a direct combination of the existing theories for the purely local and purely nonlocal settings.

The principal objective of the present paper is to bridge this gap by developing a comprehensive local regularity theory for the mixed weighted equation (1.1). More precisely, under the general growth assumption (1.2) on the right-hand side, we establish local boundedness of weak subsolutions, lower semicontinuity, weak Harnack inequalities for weak supersolutions, Harnack inequalities, and local Hölder continuity of weak solutions. To the best of our knowledge, these results are the first of their kind for mixed local and nonlocal weighted equations. In particular, they are new even in the linear casep=2p=2, under the assumptionw∈A2w\in A_{2}, and for the homogeneous equationℱ≡0\mathcal{F}\equiv 0. Consequently, the present work provides the first systematic regularity theory for mixed local and nonlocal weighted equations and extends, within a unified framework, the existing regularity theories for both purely local and purely nonlocal weighted problems.

## 1.2Main results

The main results of this article are stated below. The first one is the following local boundedness result for weak subsolutions. The main ingredients are Lemma2.9, Lemma3.3and the iteration Lemma2.12.

Supposeℱ\mathcal{F}satisfies (1.2) wheref/w,g/w∈Llocq​(Ω,w){f}/{w},{g}/{w}\in L^{q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega,w)withq>κ′q>\kappa^{\prime}, andf,gf,gsatisfy condition (1.3). Supposeℱ\mathcal{F}satisfies (1.2) wheref/w,g/w∈Llocq​(Ω,w){f}/{w},{g}/{w}\in L^{q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega,w)withq>κ′q>\kappa^{\prime}, andf,gf,gsatisfy condition (1.3). Letα>0\alpha>0anduube a weak subsolution of (1.1), which is bounded above inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}. Thenuuis upper semicontinuous inΩ\Omega.

## Remark 1.9.

We remark that the integrability conditionq>nq>nin Theorems1.4–1.6is not expected to be optimal. Indeed, in the unweighted settingw≡1w\equiv 1, by Remark2.10, we can chooseκ=n/(n−p)\kappa=n/(n-p)when1<p<n1<p<nand therefore, proceeding along the lines of the proof, all our main results are valid under the assumptionq>n/pq>n/p. We also refer to[13,30]in this concern.

## Remark 1.10.

It is worth emphasizing that all the main results above remain valid under the standing assumptionλ≥0\lambda\geq 0andΛ>0\Lambda>0, with the sole exception of the Harnack inequality (Theorem1.5), where the stronger assumptionλ>0\lambda>0is required, mainly due to the tail estimate in Lemma3.6. In particular, the local boundedness, local Hölder continuity, weak Harnack inequality, and semicontinuity results are established without any positive lower bound on the kernel. To the best of our knowledge, these results are new in the literature even in the casew≡1w\equiv 1forλ=0\lambda=0.

## Remark 1.11.

Specifically, ifα=0\alpha=0, the exact same proof gives that the corresponding estimates in Theorems1.3–1.6hold without the tail terms appearing on the right-hand sides, and the constantsCCare independent ofα\alpha,λ\lambda,Λ\Lambda, andss. Furthermore, Theorem1.7remains valid if the assumption thatuuis bounded below inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}is replaced by the weaker assumption thatuuis bounded below only inΩ\Omega.

The proofs of our main results rely on a delicate adaptation of the De Giorgi–Nash–Moser theory to the weighted framework. The main difficulty stems from the simultaneous presence of three distinct features: the Muckenhoupt weight, the interaction between the local and nonlocal operators, and the nonhomogeneous right-hand side satisfying the general growth condition (1.2). None of the existing regularity theories can be applied directly, since the local weighted theory, the weighted nonlocal theory, and the mixed unweighted theory each treat only one aspect of the present problem.

The Muckenhoupt weightwwintroduces a highly nonhomogeneous geometry, under which many of the standard tools in the classical De Giorgi theory require substantial modifications. In particular, the weighted Sobolev and Poincaré inequalities, Caccioppoli estimates, covering arguments, and iteration procedure must all be carefully adapted to the weighted setting. At the same time, the mixed nature of the operator necessitates a delicate balance between the local energy estimates and the nonlocal tail contributions throughout the iteration. An additional difficulty arises from the lower-order termℱ​(x,u)\mathcal{F}(x,u), whose dependence on the solution through the growth condition (1.2) prevents it from being treated as a simple forcing term. The assumption (1.3) plays a crucial role in controlling this contribution during the iteration process and in obtaining estimates that remain stable across different scales.

Our proofs build upon and unify several different regularity theories. More precisely, they combine the nonlocal De Giorgi-Nash-Moser theory developed in[21,22], the weighted fractional regularity theory in[5], the weighted local theory in[27], and the analytical framework for mixed local–nonlocal equations introduced in[32]. A careful synthesis of these ideas enables us to overcome the difficulties created by the interaction of the weight, the mixed diffusion, and the nonlinear lower-order term, leading to a unified regularity theory for equation (1.1).

## Organization of the paper

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we present the necessary functional setting and auxiliary results, while Section 3 is devoted to establishing the required preliminaries. Finally, in Section 4, we prove our main results.

## 2Functional setting and auxiliary results

## 2.1Functional setting

Throughout the paper, unless otherwise mentioned, we assume that1<p<∞1<p<\inftyandwwbelongs to the Muckenhoupt classApA_{p}(see[47]), which meanswwis a positive, locally integrable function onℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}such that[w]p:=supB⊂ℝn(1|B|​∫Bw​𝑑x)​(1|B|​∫Bw−1p−1​𝑑x)p−1<∞,[w]_{p}:=\sup_{B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\frac{1}{|B|}\int_{B}w\,dx\right)\left(\frac{1}{|B|}\int_{B}w^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\,dx\right)^{p-1}<\infty,(2.1)

where the supremum is taken over all ballsB⊂ℝnB\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}.

A fundamental example of anApA_{p}weight is the power weightw​(x)=|x|σ,−n<σ<n​(p−1).w(x)=|x|^{\sigma},\qquad-n<{\sigma}<n(p-1).

Forw∈Apw\in A_{p}with1<p<∞1<p<\inftyand an open subsetΩ⊂ℝn\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}, the weighted Sobolev spaceW1,p​(Ω,w)W^{1,p}(\Omega,w)is defined byW1,p​(Ω,w):={u∈Lp​(Ω,w):|∇u|∈Lp​(Ω,w)},W^{1,p}(\Omega,w):=\{u\in L^{p}(\Omega,w):|\nabla u|\in L^{p}(\Omega,w)\},

equipped with the norm‖u‖W1,p​(Ω,w):=(‖u‖Lp​(Ω,w)p+‖∇u‖Lp​(Ω,w)p)1p,\|u\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega,w)}:=\big(\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega,w)}^{p}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega,w)}^{p}\big)^{\frac{1}{p}},

whereLp​(Ω,w)L^{p}(\Omega,w)is the weighted Lebesgue space defined byLp​(Ω,w):={u:Ω→ℝ​measurable:∫Ω|u|p​w​𝑑x<∞},L^{p}(\Omega,w):=\left\{u:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\text{ measurable }:\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}w\,dx<\infty\right\},

endowed with the norm‖u‖Lp​(Ω,w):=(∫Ω|u|p​w​𝑑x)1/p.\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega,w)}:=\left(\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}w\,dx\right)^{1/p}.

We writeu∈Llocp​(Ω,w)u\in L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega,w)ifu∈Lp​(Ω′,w)u\in L^{p}({\Omega^{\prime}},w)for everyΩ′⋐Ω{\Omega^{\prime}}\Subset\Omega. Similarly, we say thatu∈Wloc1,p​(Ω,w)u\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega,w)ifu∈W1,p​(Ω′,w)u\in W^{1,p}({\Omega^{\prime}},w)for everyΩ′⋐Ω{\Omega^{\prime}}\Subset\Omega. Whenα>0\alpha>0, to deal with the mixed problems, we define the spaceW01,p​(Ω,w)W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega,w)byW01,p​(Ω,w):={u∈W1,p​(ℝn,w):u=0​in​ℝn∖Ω}.W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega,w):=\{u\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},w):u=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega\}.

Whenα=0\alpha=0, we consider the spaceW01,p​(Ω,w)W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega,w)as the closure ofCc∞​(Ω)C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)under the norm∥⋅∥W1,p​(Ω,w)\|\cdot\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega,w)}.
For more details on the weighted Sobolev spaces, we refer to[36]and the references therein.

We define the tail spaceLp​sp−1​(ℝn,w)L^{p-1}_{ps}(\mathbb{R}^{n},w)byLp​sp−1​(ℝn,w):={u∈Llocp−1​(ℝn,w):∫ℝn|u​(x)|p−1(1+|x|s​p)​w​(x)​d​xw​(Bx,0)<∞}.L^{p-1}_{ps}(\mathbb{R}^{n},w):=\Bigg\{u\in L^{p-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n},w):\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)|^{p-1}}{(1+|x|^{sp})}\frac{w(x)dx}{w(B_{x,0})}<\infty\Bigg\}.

Foru∈Lp​sp−1​(ℝn,w)u\in L^{p-1}_{ps}(\mathbb{R}^{n},w), the mixed local and nonlocal weighted tail ofuuwith respect to the ballBr​(x0)B_{r}(x_{0})forx0∈ℝnx_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}andr>0r>0is denoted byTail​(u;x0,r)\mathrm{Tail}(u;x_{0},r)and defined asTail​(u;x0,r):=(rp​∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)|u​(x)|p−1|x−x0|s​p​w​(x)​d​xw​(Bx,x0))1p−1.\displaystyle\mathrm{Tail}(u;x_{0},r):=\Bigg(r^{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|u(x)|^{p-1}}{|x-x_{0}|^{sp}}\frac{w(x)dx}{w(B_{x,x_{0}})}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}.(2.2)

We are now ready to define the notion of weak solutions to equation (1.1). To this end, we define the solution spaceXαX_{\alpha}byXα:={Wloc1,p​(Ω,w)​if​α=0,Wloc1,p​(Ω,w)∩Lp​sp−1​(ℝn,w)​if​α>0.X_{\alpha}:=\begin{cases}W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega,w)\text{ if }\alpha=0,\\
W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega,w)\cap L^{p-1}_{ps}(\mathbb{R}^{n},w)\text{ if }\alpha>0.\end{cases}(2.3)

## Definition 2.1.

Letα≥0\alpha\geq 0,0<s<1<p<∞0<s<1<p<\inftyandw∈Apw\in A_{p}. We say that a functionu∈Xαu\in X_{\alpha}is a weak subsolution (or supersolution) of equation (1.1) if for everyΩ′⋐Ω\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omegaand for every nonnegativeφ∈W01,p​(Ω′,w)\varphi\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega^{\prime},w), the following holds:∫Ω𝒜​(x,∇u)⋅∇φ​d​x+α​∫ℝn∫ℝn|u​(x)−u​(y)|p−2​(u​(x)−u​(y))​(φ​(x)−φ​(y))|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y≤(or≥)∫Ωℱ(x,u)φdx.\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\varphi\;dx\\
&+\alpha\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy\\
&\leq~(\mbox{ or }\geq)\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{F}(x,u)\varphi\;dx.\end{split}(2.4)

A functionu∈Xαu\in X_{\alpha}is said to be a weak solution to (1.1) if it is both a weak subsolution and a weak supersolution of (1.1).

## Remark 2.2.

Whenα>0\alpha>0, by Lemma2.5proved in the next subsection, Definition2.1is well stated.

## 2.2Auxiliary results

In this subsection, we establish Lemma2.5and further mention some known results, which are crucial for us. To this end, first we have the following lemma.

## Lemma 2.3.

Ifu∈Lp​sp−1​(ℝn,w)u\in L^{p-1}_{ps}(\mathbb{R}^{n},w), then for everyr>0r>0andx0∈ℝnx_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, we haveTail​(u;x0,r)<∞.\mathrm{Tail}(u;x_{0},r)<\infty.

We observe that∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)|u​(x)|p−1|x−x0|s​p​w​(x)​d​xw​(Bx,x0)=∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)|u​(x)|p−11+|x|s​p​1+|x|s​p|x−x0|s​p​w​(Bx0,0)w​(Bx,x0)​w​(x)​d​xw​(Bx0,0).\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|u(x)|^{p-1}}{|x-x_{0}|^{sp}}\frac{w(x)dx}{w(B_{x,x_{0}})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|u(x)|^{p-1}}{1+|x|^{sp}}\frac{1+|x|^{sp}}{|x-x_{0}|^{sp}}\frac{w(B_{x_{0},0})}{w(B_{x,x_{0}})}\frac{w(x)dx}{w(B_{x_{0},0})}.(2.5)

Utilizing the factsBx,0⊂B|x|+|x0|2​(x+x02)B_{x,0}\subset B_{\frac{|x|+|x_{0}|}{2}}(\frac{x+x_{0}}{2})andBx,x0⊂B|x|+|x0|2​(x+x02)B_{x,x_{0}}\subset B_{\frac{|x|+|x_{0}|}{2}}(\frac{x+x_{0}}{2})along with Lemma2.6, we obtainw​(Bx0,0)w​(Bx,x0)\displaystyle\frac{w(B_{x_{0},0})}{w(B_{x,x_{0}})}=w​(Bx0,0)w​(B|x|+|x0|2​(x+x02))​w​(B|x|+|x0|2​(x+x02))w​(Bx,x0)\displaystyle=\frac{w(B_{x_{0},0})}{w(B_{\frac{|x|+|x_{0}|}{2}}(\frac{x+x_{0}}{2}))}\frac{w(B_{\frac{|x|+|x_{0}|}{2}}(\frac{x+x_{0}}{2}))}{w(B_{x,x_{0}})}≤c​[w]p​(|Bx0,0||B|x|+|x0|2​(x+x02)|)σ​(|B|x|+|x0|2​(x+x02)||Bx,x0|)p\displaystyle\leq c[w]_{p}\Big(\frac{|B_{x_{0},0}|}{|B_{\frac{|x|+|x_{0}|}{2}}(\frac{x+x_{0}}{2})|}\Big)^{\sigma}\Big(\frac{|B_{\frac{|x|+|x_{0}|}{2}}(\frac{x+x_{0}}{2})|}{|B_{x,x_{0}}|}\Big)^{p}≤c​[w]p​(|x0||x|+|x0|)n​σ​(|x|+|x0||x−x0|)n​p\displaystyle\leq c[w]_{p}\Big(\frac{|x_{0}|}{|x|+|x_{0}|}\Big)^{n\sigma}\Big(\frac{|x|+|x_{0}|}{|x-x_{0}|}\Big)^{np}≤c​[w]p​(1+2​|x0|r)n​p<∞.\displaystyle\leq c[w]_{p}\bigg(1+\frac{2|x_{0}|}{r}\bigg)^{np}<\infty.(2.7)

Using (2.6) and (2.2) in (2.5), we obtainTail​(u;x0,r)<∞\mathrm{Tail}(u;x_{0},r)<\infty.

for everyu∈C0∞​(BR)u\in C^{\infty}_{0}(B_{R})and1≤k≤nn−1+η1\leq k\leq\frac{n}{n-1}+\eta.

There exists positive constantsccandθ\theta, depending onn,p,[w]pn,p,[w]_{p}, such that, for all Lipschitz functionuuonBR¯\overline{B_{R}}and for every1≤k≤nn−1+θ1\leq k\leq\frac{n}{n-1}+\theta,(⨏BR|u−uBR|k​p​w​𝑑x)1k​p≤c​R​(⨏BR|∇u|p​w​𝑑x)1p.\Big(\fint_{B_{R}}|u-u_{B_{R}}|^{kp}w\;dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{kp}}\leq cR\Big(\fint_{B_{R}}|\nabla u|^{p}w\;dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Lett0≥0t_{0}\geq 0andh:[t0,t1]→[0,∞)h:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to[0,\infty)be a nonnegative bounded function such that for everyt0≤t<s≤t1t_{0}\leq t<s\leq t_{1},h​(t)≤θ​h​(s)+A(s−t)τ+B,h(t)\leq\theta h(s)+\frac{A}{(s-t)^{\tau}}+B,

for some constantsθ∈(0,1),τ>0,A>0,B>0\theta\in(0,1),\tau>0,\;A>0,\;B>0. Then there exists a constantC=C​(θ,τ)>0C=C(\theta,\tau)>0, such thath​(ρ)≤C​(A(R−ρ)τ+B),h(\rho)\leq C\Big(\frac{A}{(R-\rho)^{\tau}}+B\Big),

for everyt0≤ρ<R≤t1t_{0}\leq\rho<R\leq t_{1}.

We begin by recalling the following Krylov–Safonov type[42]covering lemma for a doubling measureww. We use the version which can be proved similarly as in[41, Lemma 7.2].

## Lemma 2.14.

Letwwbe a doubling measure. LetE⊂Br​(x0)E\subset B_{r}(x_{0})be aww-measurable set andδ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1). DefineEδ:=∪0<ρ≤2​r3{Br​(x0)∩B3​ρ​(x):x∈Br​(x0),w​(E∩B3​ρ​(x))>δ​w​(Bρ​(x))}.E_{\delta}:=\cup_{0<\rho\leq\frac{2r}{3}}\{B_{r}(x_{0})\cap B_{3\rho}(x):x\in B_{r}(x_{0}),w(E\cap B_{3\rho}(x))>\delta w(B_{\rho}(x))\}.

Then, eitherEδ=Br​(x0)E_{\delta}=B_{r}(x_{0})orw​(Eδ)≥w​(E)δ​c​(w)w(E_{\delta})\geq\frac{w(E)}{\delta c(w)}, wherec​(w)c(w)is the doubling constant for the measureww.

## 3Preliminary results

## 3.1Energy and tail estimates

In this subsection, we establish several energy estimates and a tail estimate. We first prove that positive truncations of weak subsolutions are again weak subsolutions. This fact is a key ingredient in the proof of the local Hölder continuity result in Theorem1.4.

## Lemma 3.1.

Letuube a weak subsolution of (1.1), thenu+u_{+}is a weak subsolution ofℳα​u−|g|​|u|p−2​u=|f|​in​Ω.\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\,u-|g||u|^{p-2}u=|f|\text{ in }\Omega.(3.1)

## Proof.

We defineum​(x):=min⁡{m​(u​(x))+,1}u_{m}(x):=\min\{m(u(x))_{+},1\}, which converges toχsupp​(u+)\chi_{\mathrm{supp}(u_{+})}pointwise almost everywhere inΩ\Omega. LetΩ′⋐Ω\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omegaandφ∈W01,p​(Ω′,w)\varphi\in W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega^{\prime},w)be a nonnegative function. Sinceuuis a weak subsolution of (1.1), incorporatingψ=φ​um\psi=\varphi u_{m}as a test function in (2.4) and using the property (1.2), we getI1+α​I2+I3≤I4,\displaystyle I_{1}+\alpha\,I_{2}+I_{3}\leq I_{4},(3.2)

whereI1:=∫Ω′𝒜​(x,∇u)⋅∇(φ​um)⁡d​x,I_{1}:=\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla(\varphi u_{m})\;dx,I2:=∫ℝn∫ℝn|u​(x)−u​(y)|p−2​(u​(x)−u​(y))​(um​(x)​φ​(x)−um​(y)​φ​(y))|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y,I_{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))(u_{m}(x)\varphi(x)-u_{m}(y)\varphi(y))}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy,I3:=−∫Ω′|g|​|u|p−1​um​φ​𝑑xandI4:=∫Ω′|f|​um​φ​𝑑x.I_{3}:=-\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|g||u|^{p-1}u_{m}\varphi\;dx\quad\mbox{ and }\quad I_{4}:=\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|u_{m}\varphi\;dx.

Using the definition ofumu_{m}and the properties of𝒜\mathcal{A}, we deduce thatI1\displaystyle I_{1}≥∫Ω′um​𝒜​(x,∇u)⋅∇φ​d​x+∫Ω′φ​𝒜​(x,∇u)⋅∇um​d​x\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}u_{m}\mathcal{A}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\varphi\;dx+\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}\varphi\mathcal{A}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla u_{m}\;dx≥∫Ω′um​𝒜​(x,∇u)⋅∇φ​d​x+m​C1​∫{x∈Ω′:u+​(x)≤1m}φ​|∇u+|p​w​𝑑x\displaystyle{\geq}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}u_{m}\mathcal{A}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\varphi\;dx+mC_{1}\int_{\{x\in\Omega^{\prime}:u_{+}(x)\leq\frac{1}{m}\}}\varphi|\nabla u_{+}|^{p}~w\;dx≥∫Ω′um​𝒜​(x,∇u)⋅∇φ​d​x.\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}u_{m}\mathcal{A}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\varphi\;dx.(3.3)

Along the lines of proof of[32, estimate (2.11)-(2.12), page 5398]and using the Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we deduce thatlimm→∞I2≥∫ℝn∫ℝn|u+​(x)−u+​(y)|p−2​(u+​(x)−u+​(y))​(φ​(x)−φ​(y))|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y.\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}I_{2}\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u_{+}(x)-u_{+}(y)|^{p-2}(u_{+}(x)-u_{+}(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy.(3.4)

Using the factq>κ′q>\kappa^{\prime}, we obtain thatg​|u|p−1​φ∈L1​(Ω′)g|u|^{p-1}\varphi\in L^{1}(\Omega^{\prime})and hence by the Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we get∫Ω′|g|​|u|p−1​um​φ​𝑑x→∫Ω′|g|​u+p−1​φ​𝑑x​as​m→∞,\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|g||u|^{p-1}u_{m}\varphi\;dx\to\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|g|u_{+}^{p-1}\varphi\;dx\text{ as }m\to\infty,(3.5)

and∫Ω′|f|​um​φ​𝑑x→∫Ω′|f|​χsupp​(u+)​φ​𝑑x​as​m→∞.\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|u_{m}\varphi\;dx\to\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|\chi_{\mathrm{supp}(u_{+})}\varphi\;dx\text{ as }m\to\infty.(3.6)

Utilizing (3.1)-(3.6) in (3.2) and takingm→∞m\to\infty, we deduce that∫Ω′𝒜​(x,∇u+)⋅∇φ​d​x\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}(x,\nabla u_{+})\cdot\nabla\varphi\;dx+α​∫ℝn∫ℝn|u+​(x)−u+​(y)|p−2​(u+​(x)−u+​(y))​(φ​(x)−φ​(y))|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y\displaystyle+\alpha\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u_{+}(x)-u_{+}(y)|^{p-2}(u_{+}(x)-u_{+}(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy−∫Ω′|g|​u+p−1​φ​𝑑x≤∫Ω′|f|​φ​𝑑x.\displaystyle{-}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|g|u_{+}^{p-1}\varphi\;dx\leq\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}|f|\varphi\;dx.(3.7)

Consequently,u+u_{+}is a weak subsolution of (3.1).
∎

## Remark 3.2.

Ifuuis a weak supersolution of (1.1), then we observe thatv=−uv=-uis a weak subsolution ofℳα​v=𝒢​(x,v)​in​Ω,\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}v=\mathcal{G}(x,v)\text{ in }\Omega,

where𝒢​(x,v):=−ℱ​(x,−v)\mathcal{G}(x,v):=-\mathcal{F}(x,-v)with𝒢\mathcal{G}satisfying (1.2). Then by Lemma3.1, it follows thatv+=u−v_{+}=u_{-}is also a weak subsolution of (3.1).

The following energy estimate for weak subsolutions will be used repeatedly in the proofs of our main results.

## Lemma 3.3(Energy estimate for subsolutions).

SupposeBr​(x0)⋐ΩB_{r}(x_{0})\Subset\Omegaandψ∈Cc∞​(Br​(x0))\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{r}(x_{0}))is a nonnegative function. Then there exists a constantC=C​(p,Λ,α,C1,C2)>0C=C(p,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2})>0such that⨏Br​(x0)ψp​|∇v±|p​w​𝑑x≤C{⨏Br​(x0)v±p|∇ψ|pwdx+⨏Br​(x0)∫Br​(x0)(|ψ​(x)−ψ​(y)|​max⁡{v±​(x),v±​(y)}|x−y|s)p​w​(x)​w​(y)w​(Bx,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y+(supy∈supp​ψ∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)(v​(x))±p−1|x−y|s​pw​(x)w​(Bx,y)dx)⨏Br​(x0)v±ψpwdx}+⨏Br​(x0)|g|​|u|p−1​v±​ψp​𝑑x+⨏Br​(x0)|f|​v±​ψp​𝑑x,\begin{split}\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\psi^{p}|\nabla v_{\pm}|^{p}\;w~dx&\leq C\Bigg\{\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}v_{\pm}^{p}|\nabla\psi|^{p}\;w\;dx\\
&+\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\Big(\frac{|\psi(x)-\psi(y)|\max\{v_{\pm}(x),v_{\pm}(y)\}}{|x-y|^{s}}\Big)^{p}\frac{w(x)w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})}\;dx\;dy\\
&+\Big(\sup_{y\in\mathrm{supp}\,\psi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{(v(x))_{\pm}^{p-1}}{|x-y|^{sp}}\frac{w(x)}{w(B_{x,y})}\;dx\Big)\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}v_{\pm}\psi^{p}w\;dx\Bigg\}\\
&\qquad+\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|g||u|^{p-1}v_{\pm}\psi^{p}\;dx+\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|f|v_{\pm}\psi^{p}\;dx,\end{split}

holds forv+=(u−k)+v_{+}=(u-k)_{+}ifuuis a weak subsolution and forv−=(u−k)−v_{-}=(u-k)_{-}ifuuis a weak supersolution of (1.1) with anyk∈ℝk\in\mathbb{R}.

## Proof.

First, we prove the result for weak subsolutions. To this end, letuube a weak subsolution of (1.1), then incorporatingφ=v+​ψp\varphi=v_{+}\psi^{p}in (2.4), we getI1+α​I2≤I3,I_{1}+\alpha\,I_{2}\leq I_{3},(3.8)

whereI1:=∫Ω𝒜​(x,∇u)⋅∇φ​d​x,I_{1}:=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\varphi\;dx,I2:=∫ℝn∫ℝn|u​(x)−u​(y)|p−2​(u​(x)−u​(y))​(φ​(x)−φ​(y))|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y,I_{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy,

andI3:=∫Ωℱ​(x,u)​φ​𝑑x.I_{3}:=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{F}(x,u)\varphi\;dx.

Proceeding along the lines of proofs of[14, Proposition 3.1, page 14], we getI1≥C​∫Br​(x0)ψp​|∇v+|p​w​𝑑x−C​∫Br​(x0)v+p​|∇ψ|p​w​𝑑x,\begin{split}I_{1}\geq C\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\psi^{p}|\nabla v_{+}|^{p}w\;dx-C\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}v_{+}^{p}|\nabla\psi|^{p}w\;dx,\end{split}(3.9)

for some constantC=C​(p,C1,C2)>0C=C(p,C_{1},C_{2})>0. Along the lines of the proof of[22, Theorem 1.4, estimates (3.2) and (3.4) on pages 1286-1287], we obtain
andI2≥12​∫Br​(x0)∫Br​(x0)|v+(x)−v+(y)|pmax{ψ(x),ψ(y)}p|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y−C​∫Br​(x0)∫Br​(x0)|ψ(x)−ψ(y)|pmax{v+(x),v+(y)}p|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y−C​(supy∈supp​ψ∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)|v+​(x)|p−1|x−y|s​p​w​(x)w​(Bx,y)​𝑑x)​∫Br​(x0)v+​(y)​ψ​(y)p​w​(y)​𝑑y,\begin{split}I_{2}&\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|v_{+}(x)-v_{+}(y)|^{p}\max\{\psi(x),\psi(y)\}^{p}}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy\\
&-C\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|\psi(x)-\psi(y)|^{p}\max\{v_{+}(x),v_{+}(y)\}^{p}}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy\\
&-{C\Big(\sup_{y\in\mathrm{supp}\,\psi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|v_{+}(x)|^{p-1}}{|x-y|^{sp}}\frac{w(x)}{w(B_{x,y})}\;dx\Big)\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}v_{+}(y)\psi(y)^{p}w(y)\;dy},\end{split}(3.10)

for some constantC=C​(p,Λ,α)>0C=C(p,\Lambda,\alpha)>0. Combining the above two estimates (3.9) and (3.10) in (3.8) together with the fact0≤K​(x,y)≤Λ​w​(x)​w​(y)w​(Bx,y)0\leq K(x,y)\leq\frac{\Lambda w(x)w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})}and the hypothesis (1.2), we obtain the desired estimate (3.3).

For weak supersolutions, the result holds by observing the fact that, ifuuis a weak supersolution of (1.1), thenv=−uv=-uis a weak subsolution ofℳα​v=𝒢​(x,v)​in​Ω,\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\,v=\mathcal{G}(x,v)\text{ in }\Omega,

where𝒢​(x,v):=−ℱ​(x,−v)\mathcal{G}(x,v):=-\mathcal{F}(x,-v), with𝒢\mathcal{G}satisfying (1.2).
∎

The following energy estimate for weak supersolutions is a key ingredient in the proof of the reverse Hölder inequality, which subsequently leads to the weak Harnack inequality established in Theorem1.6.

## Lemma 3.4(Energy estimate for supersolutions).

Supposeuuis a weak supersolution of (1.1) such thatu≥0u\geq 0inBR​(x0)⋐ΩB_{R}(x_{0})\Subset\Omega. Let0<r≤3​R40<r\leq\frac{3R}{4}andψ∈Cc∞​(Br​(x0))\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{r}(x_{0}))be a nonnegative function. Then for any1<η<p1<\eta<pand anyd>0d>0, there exists a constantC=C​(n,p,Λ,α,C1,C2,[w]p)>0C=C(n,p,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2},[w]_{p})>0such that∫Br​(x0)ψp|∇v|pwdx≤C[(p−η)p(η−1)p∫Br​(x0)vp|∇ψ|pwdx+(p−η)p(η−1)p∫Br​(x0)∫Br​(x0)|ψ​(x)−ψ​(y)|p|x−y|s​pmax{v(x),v(y)}pK(x,y)dxdy+(p−η)p(η−1){(supx∈supp​ψ∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)1|x−y|s​pw​(y)w​(Bx,y)dy+d1−pR−pTailp−1(u−;x0,R))×∫Br​(x0)ψpvpwdx+∫Br​(x0)|g|up−1ψp(u+d)1−ηdx+∫Br​(x0)|f|ψp(u+d)1−ηdx}]\begin{split}&\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\psi^{p}|\nabla v|^{p}~w\;dx\leq C\Bigg[\frac{(p-\eta)^{p}}{(\eta-1)^{p}}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}v^{p}|\nabla\psi|^{p}w\;dx\\
&+\frac{(p-\eta)^{p}}{(\eta-1)^{p}}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}{\frac{|\psi(x)-\psi(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{sp}}\max\{v(x),v(y)\}^{p}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy}\\
&+\frac{(p-\eta)^{p}}{(\eta-1)}\Big\{\Big(\sup_{x\in\mathrm{supp}\,\psi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{sp}}\frac{w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})}\;dy+d^{1-p}R^{-p}\mathrm{Tail}^{p-1}(u_{-};x_{0},R)\Big)\\
&\times\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\psi^{p}v^{p}w\;dx+\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|g|u^{p-1}\psi^{p}(u+d)^{1-\eta}\;dx+\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|f|\psi^{p}(u+d)^{1-\eta}\;dx\Big\}\Bigg]\end{split}

withv=(u+d)p−ηpv=(u+d)^{\frac{p-\eta}{p}}. Here,Tail\mathrm{Tail}is given in (2.2).

## Proof.

Sinceuuis a weak supersolution of (1.1), choosingφ=ψp​(u+d)1−η\varphi=\psi^{p}(u+d)^{1-\eta}in (2.4), we arrive atI1+α​I2≥I3,I_{1}+\alpha\,I_{2}\geq I_{3},(3.11)

whereI1:=∫Ω𝒜​(x,∇u)⋅∇φ​d​x,I_{1}:=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{A}(x,\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\varphi\;dx,I2:=∫ℝn∫ℝn|u​(x)−u​(y)|p−2​(u​(x)−u​(y))​(φ​(x)−φ​(y))|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y,I_{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy,

andI3:=∫Ωℱ​(x,u)​φ​𝑑x.I_{3}:=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{F}(x,u)\varphi\;dx.

Following the proof of[32, estimate (3.10) on page 5401], we obtainI1≤−(η−12)​(pp−η)p​∫Br​(x0)|∇v|p​ψp​w​𝑑x+C(η−1)p−1​∫Br​(x0)|∇ψ|p​vp​w​𝑑x,\displaystyle I_{1}\leq-\bigg(\frac{\eta-1}{2}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{p}{p-\eta}\bigg)^{p}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla v|^{p}\psi^{p}w\;dx+\frac{C}{(\eta-1)^{p-1}}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla\psi|^{p}v^{p}w\;dx,(3.12)

for some constantC=C​(p,C1,C2)>0C=C(p,{C_{1},C_{2})}>0.
We divideI2I_{2}into two parts as follows:I2=I21+I22,\displaystyle I_{2}=I_{21}+I_{22},(3.13)

withI21:=∫Br​(x0)∫Br​(x0)|u​(x)−u​(y)|p−2​(u​(x)−u​(y))​(φ​(x)−φ​(y))|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑yI_{21}:=\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy

andI22:=2​∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)∫Br​(x0)|u​(x)−u​(y)|p−2​(u​(x)−u​(y))​(φ​(x)−φ​(y))|x−y|s​p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y.I_{22}:=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^{p-2}(u(x)-u(y))(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))}{|x-y|^{sp}}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy.

Proceeding as in the proof of[21, Lemma 5.1, pages 1830–1833], we obtainI21\displaystyle I_{21}≤−C​(p,η,C1,C2)​∫Br​(x0)∫Br​(x0)|v​(x)−v​(y)|p|x−y|s​p​|ψ​(y)|p​K​(x,y)​𝑑x​𝑑y\displaystyle\leq-{C(p,\eta,C_{1},C_{2})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|v(x)-v(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{sp}}|\psi(y)|^{p}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy+C​(p,C1,C2)(η−1)p−1∫Br​(x0)∫Br​(x0)|ψ​(x)−ψ​(y)|p|x−y|s​pmax{v(x),v(y)}pK(x,y)dxdy.\displaystyle+\frac{{C(p,C_{1},C_{2})}}{(\eta-1)^{p-1}}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{|\psi(x)-\psi(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{sp}}\max\{v(x),v(y)\}^{p}K(x,y)\;dx\;dy.(3.14)

Moreover, proceeding as in the proof of[21, Lemma 5.1, page 1830], we obtainI22≤C(p,Λ)(\displaystyle I_{22}\leq C(p,\Lambda)\Bigg(supx∈supp​ψ∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)1|x−y|s​p​w​(y)w​(Bx,y)​𝑑y\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathrm{supp}\,\psi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{sp}}\frac{w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})}\;dy+d1−p∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)(u​(y))−p−1|x−y|s​pw​(y)w​(Bx,y)dy)∫Br​(x0)ψpvpwdx.\displaystyle+d^{1-p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{{(u(y))_{-}^{p-1}}}{|x-y|^{sp}}\frac{w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})}\;dy\Bigg)\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\psi^{p}v^{p}w\;dx.(3.15)

In the last estimate above, we have also used the factK​(x,y)≤Λ​w​(x)​w​(y)w​(Bx,y)K(x,y)\leq\frac{\Lambda w(x)w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})}. For0<r≤3​R40<r\leq\frac{3R}{4}andy∈ℝn∖BR​(x0)y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0}), we observe that|y−x0||x−y|≤1+|x−x0||x−y|≤1+rR−r≤4.\displaystyle\frac{|y-x_{0}|}{|x-y|}\leq 1+\frac{|x-x_{0}|}{|x-y|}\leq 1+\frac{r}{R-r}\leq 4.(3.16)

Furthermore, sinceBx,y∪Bx0,y⊂B2​|y−x0|​(x0)B_{x,y}\cup B_{x_{0},y}\subset B_{2|y-x_{0}|}(x_{0}), using Lemma2.6, we havew​(Bx0,y)w​(Bx,y)\displaystyle\frac{w(B_{x_{0},y})}{w(B_{x,y})}=w​(Bx0,y)w​(B2​|y−x0|​(x0))​w​(B2​|y−x0|​(x0))w​(Bx,y)\displaystyle=\frac{w(B_{x_{0},y})}{w(B_{2|y-x_{0}|}(x_{0}))}\frac{w(B_{2|y-x_{0}|}(x_{0}))}{w(B_{x,y})}≤C​(|Bx0,y||B2​|y−x0|​(x0)|)σ​(|B2​|y−x0|​(x0)||Bx,y|)p≤C​(|y−x0||x−y|)n​p≤C,\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\frac{|B_{x_{0},y}|}{|B_{2|y-x_{0}|}(x_{0})|}\Big)^{\sigma}\Big(\frac{|B_{2|y-x_{0}|}(x_{0})|}{|B_{x,y}|}\Big)^{p}\leq C\Big(\frac{|y-x_{0}|}{|x-y|}\Big)^{np}\leq C,(3.17)

for some constantC=C​(n,p,[w]p)>0C=C(n,p,[w]_{p})>0.
Therefore, we deduce that∫ℝn∖Br​(x0)(u​(y))−p−1|x−y|s​p​w​(y)w​(Bx,y)​𝑑y\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0})}\frac{(u(y))_{-}^{p-1}}{|x-y|^{sp}}\frac{w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})}\;dy}≤∫ℝn∖BR​(x0)(u​(y))−p−1|x0−y|s​p​w​(y)w​(Bx0,y)​𝑑y\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}\frac{(u(y))_{-}^{p-1}}{|x_{0}-y|^{sp}}\frac{w(y)}{w(B_{x_{0},y})}\;dy=R−p​Tailp−1​(u−;x0,R).\displaystyle=R^{-p}\mathrm{Tail}^{p-1}(u_{-};x_{0},R).(3.18)

Combining the estimates (3.12), (3.1), (3.1), (3.1) in (3.11) alongside the fact0≤K​(x,y)≤Λ​w​(x)​w​(y)w​(Bx,y)0\leq K(x,y)\leq\frac{\Lambda w(x)w(y)}{w(B_{x,y})}and the hypothesis (1.2), we obtain the desired estimate.

The following logarithmic estimate for weak supersolutions is one of the key ingredients in the proof of the expansion of positivity property established in Lemma3.7.

## Lemma 3.5(Logarithmic estimate for supersolutions).

Letd>0d>0anduube a weak supersolution to (1.1) such thatu≥0u\geq 0inBR​(x0)⋐ΩB_{R}(x_{0})\Subset\Omega. Then, there exists a constantC=C​(n,p,s,Λ,α,C1,C2,[w]p,M)>0C=C(n,p,s,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2},[w]_{p},M)>0such that⨏Br​(x0)|∇log⁡(u+d)|p​w​𝑑x\displaystyle\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}|\nabla\log(u+d)|^{p}w\;dx≤Crp​{1+d1−p​(rR)p​Tailp−1​(u−;x0,R)+d1−p​rp​(⨏B2​r​(x0)(|f|w)q​w​𝑑x)1q},\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{r^{p}}\Big\{1+d^{1-p}(\frac{r}{R})^{p}\mathrm{Tail}^{p-1}(u_{-};x_{0},R)+d^{1-p}r^{p}\Big(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\Big(\frac{|f|}{w}\Big)^{q}w\;dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\Big\},(3.19)

for every0<r≤10<r\leq 1withr<R2r<\frac{R}{2}. Furthermore, ifd≥(rR)p′​Tail​(u−;x0,R)+rp′​(⨏B2​r​(x0)(|f|w)q​w​𝑑x)1q​(p−1),d\geq\Big(\frac{r}{R}\Big)^{p^{\prime}}\mathrm{Tail}(u_{-};x_{0},R)+r^{p^{\prime}}\Big(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\Big(\frac{|f|}{w}\Big)^{q}w\;dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{q(p-1)}},

for some constantC=C​(n,p,Λ,C1,C2,[w]p)>0C=C(n,p,\Lambda,C_{1},C_{2},[w]_{p})>0. ## 3.2Expansion of positivity

In this subsection, our primary objective is to establish the expansion of positivity property for weak supersolutions. This property plays a crucial role in proving the local Hölder continuity result stated in Theorem1.4. Furthermore, it is instrumental in deriving the preliminary version of the weak Harnack inequality presented in Lemma4.1, which subsequently serves as a key step in the proofs of the Harnack and weak Harnack inequalities.

## Lemma 3.7.

Letuube a weak supersolution of (1.1) such thatu≥0u\geq 0inBR​(x0)⋐ΩB_{R}(x_{0})\Subset\Omega. Letk≥0k\geq 0and assume that there exists a constantτ∈(0,1)\tau\in(0,1)such thatw({B2​r(x0):u≥k})≥τw(B2​r(x0),\displaystyle w(\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):u\geq k\})\geq\tau w(B_{2r}(x_{0}),(3.32)

for some0<r≤10<r\leq 1withr<R4r<\frac{R}{4}. Then there exists a constantζ=ζ(n,p,s,q,κ,Λ,α,C1,C2,[w]p,\zeta=\zeta(n,p,s,q,\kappa,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2},[w]_{p},M,τ)∈(0,14)M,\tau)\in(0,\frac{1}{4})such thatinfBr​(x0)u≥ζ​k−(rR)p′​Tail​(u−;x0,R)−rp′​(⨏B2​r​(x0)(|f|w)q​w​𝑑x)1q​(p−1),\displaystyle\inf_{B_{r}(x_{0})}u\geq\zeta k-\Big(\frac{r}{R}\Big)^{p^{\prime}}\mathrm{Tail}(u_{-};x_{0},R)-r^{p^{\prime}}\Big(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\Big(\frac{|f|}{w}\Big)^{q}w\;dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{q(p-1)}},(3.33)

whereMMandTail\mathrm{Tail}are given in (1.3) and (2.2), respectively.

## Proof.

We prove the result into the following two steps.
Step-I:We show that for everyδ∈(0,14)\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{4})and for everyϵ>0\epsilon>0, there exists a constantC=C​(n,p,s,q,Λ,α,C1,C2,[w]p,M)>0C=C(n,p,s,q,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2},[w]_{p},M)>0such thatw​({B2​r​(x0):u≤2​δ​k−d2−ϵ})w​(B2​r​(x0))≤Cτ​log⁡(12​δ),\displaystyle\frac{w(\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):u\leq 2\delta k-\frac{d}{2}-\epsilon\})}{w(B_{2r}(x_{0}))}\leq\frac{C}{\tau\log\big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\big)},(3.34)

whered:=(rR)p′​Tail​(u−;x0,R)+rp′​(⨏B2​r​(x0)(|f|w)q​w​𝑑x)1q​(p−1).d:=\Big(\frac{r}{R}\Big)^{p^{\prime}}\mathrm{Tail}(u_{-};x_{0},R)+r^{p^{\prime}}\Big(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\Big(\frac{|f|}{w}\Big)^{q}w\;dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{q(p-1)}}.

To this end, we denotetϵ=d2+ϵ>0t_{\epsilon}=\frac{d}{2}+\epsilon>0. Due to Lemma3.5, we have⨏B2​r​(x0)|∇log⁡(u+tϵ)|p​w​𝑑x≤Crp,\displaystyle\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|\nabla\log(u+t_{\epsilon})|^{p}w\;dx\leq\frac{C}{r^{p}},(3.35)

for some constantC=C​(n,p,s,Λ,α,C1,C2,[w]p,M)>0C=C(n,p,s,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2},[w]_{p},M)>0. Now we definev:=min⁡[log⁡(12​δ),{log⁡(k+tϵu​(x)+tϵ)}+].v:=\min\Big[\log\Big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\Big),\Big\{\log\Big(\frac{k+t_{\epsilon}}{u(x)+t_{\epsilon}}\Big)\Big\}_{+}\Big].

Note that,v=0v=0if and only ifu≥ku\geq k. Moreover,⨏B2​r​(x0)|∇v|p​w​𝑑x≤⨏B2​r​(x0)|∇log⁡(u+tϵ)|p​w​𝑑x≤Crp,\displaystyle\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|\nabla v|^{p}w\;dx\leq\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|\nabla\log(u+t_{\epsilon})|^{p}w\;dx\leq\frac{C}{r^{p}},(3.36)

for some constantC=C​(n,p,s,Λ,α,C1,C2,[w]p,M)>0C=C(n,p,s,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2},[w]_{p},M)>0.
Using Hölder’s inequality, Theorem2.11and (3.36), we deduce⨏B2​r​(x0)|v−(v)B2​r​(x0)|​w​𝑑x≤(⨏B2​r​(x0)|v−(v)B2​r​(x0)|p​w​𝑑x)1p\displaystyle\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|v-(v)_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|w\;dx\leq\Big(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|v-(v)_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|^{p}w\;dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}≤C​r​(⨏B2​r​(x0)|∇v|p​w​𝑑x)1p\displaystyle\leq Cr\Big(\fint_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|\nabla v|^{p}w\;dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}≤C,\displaystyle\leq C,(3.37)

for some constantC=C​(n,p,s,Λ,α,C1,C2,[w]p,M)>0C=C(n,p,s,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2},[w]_{p},M)>0.
Thanks to (3.32), we havelog⁡(12​δ)\displaystyle\log\Big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\Big)=1w​({B2​r​(x0):v=0})​∫{B2​r​(x0):v=0}(log⁡(12​δ)−v)​w​𝑑x\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w(\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):v=0\})}\int_{\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):v=0\}}\Big(\log\Big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\Big)-v\Big)w\;dx≤1w​({B2​r​(x0):u≥k})​∫B2​r​(x0)(log⁡(12​δ)−v)​w​𝑑x\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{w(\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):u\geq k\})}\int_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\Big(\log\Big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\Big)-v\Big)w\;dx≤1τ​w​(B2​r​(x0))​∫B2​r​(x0)(log⁡(12​δ)−v)​w​𝑑x=1τ​(log⁡(12​δ)−(v)B2​r​(x0)).\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\tau w(B_{2r}(x_{0}))}\int_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\Big(\log\Big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\Big)-v\Big)w\;dx=\frac{1}{\tau}\Big(\log\Big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\Big)-(v)_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\Big).(3.38)

Integrating both sides of (3.2) overw​({B2​r​(x0):v=log⁡(12​δ)})w(\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):v=\log\big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\big)\}), and using (3.2), one haslog⁡(12​δ)​w​({B2​r​(x0):v=log⁡(12​δ)})\displaystyle\log\Big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\Big)w\Big(\Big\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):v=\log\Big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\Big)\Big\}\Big)≤1τ​∫w​({B2​r​(x0):v=log⁡(12​δ)})(log⁡(12​δ)−(v)B2​r​(x0))​w​𝑑x\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{w\big(\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):v=\log\big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\big)\big\}\big)}\Big(\log\Big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\Big)-(v)_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\Big)w\;dx=1τ​∫w​({B2​r​(x0):v=log⁡(12​δ)})(v−(v)B2​r​(x0))​w​𝑑x\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{w\big(\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):v=\log\big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\big)\big\}\big)}\Big(v-(v)_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}\Big)w\;dx≤1τ​∫B2​r​(x0)|v−(v)B2​r​(x0)|​w​𝑑x≤Cτ​w​(B2​r​(x0)),\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|v-(v)_{B_{2r}(x_{0})}|w\;dx\leq\frac{C}{\tau}w(B_{2r}(x_{0})),(3.39)

which yieldsw​({B2​r​(x0):v=log⁡(12​δ)})w​(B2​r​(x0))≤Cτ​log⁡(12​δ),\displaystyle\frac{w(\{B_{2r}(x_{0}):v=\log\big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\big)\})}{w(B_{2r}(x_{0}))}\leq\frac{C}{\tau\log(\frac{1}{2\delta})},(3.40)

for some constantC=C​(n,p,s,Λ,α,C1,C2,[w]p,M)>0C=C(n,p,s,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2},[w]_{p},M)>0.
Finally, we observe thatv​(x)=log⁡(12​δ)v(x)=\log\big(\frac{1}{2\delta}\big)wheneveru≤2​δ​k−d2−ϵu\leq 2\delta k-\frac{d}{2}-\epsilon. Thus, (3.40) gives (3.34).
Step-II:We aim to show that for everyϵ>0\epsilon>0, there exists a constantζ=ζ​(n,p,s,q,Λ,α,C1,C2,[w]p,M)∈(0,14)\zeta=\zeta(n,p,s,q,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},\\

## 3.3De-Giorgi type lemma

This subsection is mainly devoted to the proof of the semicontinuity result stated in Theorem1.7. To this end, we establish Proposition3.8, by adapting the argument in the proof of[43, Theorem 2.1]. This yields a semicontinuous representative under a suitable De Giorgi-type result in Lemma3.9.

LetBρ​(x0)⋐ΩB_{\rho}(x_{0})\Subset\Omegaand introduce the real numbersa,La,Landμ−\mu^{-}satisfyinga∈(0,1),L>0,μ−≤infBρ​(x0)u.a\in(0,1),\quad L>0,\quad\mu^{-}\leq\inf_{B_{\rho}(x_{0})}\,u.(3.53)

We say thatuusatisfies the property(𝒟)(\mathcal{D}), if there exists a constantσ∈(0,1){\sigma}\in(0,1)depending ona,μ−,La,\mu^{-},Land other data (may depend on the partial differential equation and will be made
precise in Lemma3.9), but independent ofρ\rho, such that ifw​({Bρ​(x0):u≤μ−+L})≤σ​w​(Bρ​(x0)),w(\{B_{\rho}(x_{0}):u\leq\mu^{-}+L\})\leq{\sigma}\,w(B_{\rho}(x_{0})),

thenu≥μ−+a​Lu\geq\mu^{-}+aLinBρ2​(x0)B_{{\frac{\rho}{2}}}(x_{0}).
Further, we defineℱ:={x∈Ω:|u​(x)|​<∞,limr→0⨏Br​(x)|​u​(x)−u​(y)|w​(y)​d​y=0}.\mathcal{F}:=\Bigg\{x\in\Omega:|u(x)|<\infty,\quad\lim_{r\to 0}\fint_{B_{r}(x)}|u(x)-u(y)|w(y)dy=0\Bigg\}.

Using Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem, it follows that|ℱ|=|Ω||\mathcal{F}|=|\Omega|, (see[26, Theorem 1.33]).

## Proposition 3.8.

Supposeuuis a locally integrable and locally essentially bounded below function
inΩ\Omegasatisfying the property(𝒟)(\mathcal{D}). Thenu​(x)=u∗​(x)u(x)=u_{*}(x)for everyx∈ℱx\in\mathcal{F}, whereu∗​(x):=limr→0infBr​(x)u.u_{*}(x):=\lim_{r\to 0}\inf_{B_{r}(x)}u.

In particular,u∗u_{*}is a lower semicontinuous representative ofuuinΩ\Omega.

## Proof.

First we observe that, for everyx∈ℱx\in\mathcal{F},u​(x)=limr→0⨏Br​(x)u​(y)​w​(y)​𝑑y≥limr→0infBr​(x)u=u∗​(x).\displaystyle u(x)=\lim_{r\to 0}\fint_{B_{r}(x)}u(y)w(y)dy\geq\lim_{r\to 0}\inf_{B_{r}(x)}u=u_{*}(x).

To prove the converse, let us assume by contradiction that there exists a pointx0∈ℱx_{0}\in\mathcal{F}such thatu​(x0)>u∗​(x0)u(x_{0})>u_{*}(x_{0}). We fixR>0R>0such thatBR​(x0)⋐ΩB_{R}(x_{0})\Subset\Omegaand letμ−,L\mu^{-},Lsatisfyμ−:=infBR​(x0)u≤u∗​(x0)<μ−+L<u​(x0)\mu^{-}:=\inf_{B_{R}(x_{0})}\,u\leq u_{*}(x_{0})<\mu^{-}+L<u(x_{0}). Now after fixing the quantitiesu∗​(x0),μ−u_{*}(x_{0}),\mu^{-}andLL, we choosea∈(0,1)a\in(0,1), such thatu∗​(x0)<μ−+a​L,i.e.​u∗​(x0)−μ−L<a<1.\displaystyle u_{*}(x_{0})<\mu^{-}+aL,\text{ i.e. }\frac{u_{*}(x_{0})-\mu^{-}}{L}<a<1.(3.54)

Now with suchaabeing fixed in (3.54), we determineν\nudepending ona,μ−,La,\mu^{-},Land other given data but independent ofrras per the property(𝒟)(\mathcal{D}). Next, we claim that there must be someρ∈(0,R)\rho\in(0,{R})such thatw​({Bρ​(x0):u≤μ−+L})<σ​w​(Bρ​(x0)).w(\{B_{\rho}(x_{0}):u\leq\mu^{-}+L\})<\sigma w(B_{\rho}(x_{0})).(3.55)

Otherwise, we would arrive at the estimate∫Bρ​(x0)|u​(x0)−u​(y)|​w​(y)​𝑑y\displaystyle\int_{B_{\rho}(x_{0})}|u(x_{0})-u(y)|w(y)\;dy≥∫{y∈Bρ​(x0):u​(y)≤μ−+L}(u​(x0)−μ−−L)​w​(y)​𝑑y\displaystyle\geq\int_{\{y\in B_{\rho}(x_{0}):u(y)\leq\mu^{-}+L\}}(u(x_{0})-\mu^{-}-L)w(y)\;dy=(u​(x)−μ−−L)​w​({y∈Bρ​(x0):u​(y)≤μ−+L})\displaystyle=(u(x)-\mu^{-}-L)w(\{y\in B_{\rho}(x_{0}):u(y)\leq\mu^{-}+L\})≥σ​(u​(x0)−μ−−L)​w​(Bρ​(x0)),\displaystyle\geq\sigma(u(x_{0})-\mu^{-}-L)w(B_{\rho}(x_{0})),

for allρ∈(0,R)\rho\in(0,R), which is a contradiction to the fact thatx0∈ℱx_{0}\in\mathcal{F}, sinceν\nudoes not depend onρ\rho. Now by the property(𝒟)(\mathcal{D}), the measure information (3.55) and the choice ofaain (3.54) imply thatu≥μ−+a​L>u∗​(x0)​in​Bρ2​(x0),u\geq\mu^{-}+aL>u_{*}(x_{0})\text{ in }B_{{\frac{\rho}{2}}}(x_{0}),

which contradicts the definition ofu∗​(x0)u_{*}(x_{0}). As a result, we must have,u∗​(x)≥u​(x)u_{*}(x)\geq u(x)for everyx∈ℱx\in\mathcal{F}.
∎

## Lemma 3.9(De-Giorgi type result).

Supposeℱ\mathcal{F}satisfies (1.2) wheref/w,g/w∈Llocq​(Ω,w){f}/{w},{g}/{w}\in L^{q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega,w)withq>κ′q>\kappa^{\prime}, andf,gf,gsatisfy condition (1.3). Letuube a weak supersolution of (1.1) which is bounded below inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}. Suppose thatBr​(x0)⋐ΩB_{r}(x_{0})\Subset\Omegawithr∈(0,1]r\in(0,1],μ1≤infBr​(x0)u\mu_{1}\leq\inf_{B_{r}(x_{0})}u,μ2≤infℝnu\mu_{2}\leq\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u, andδ∈(0,1),L>0\delta\in(0,1),\;L>0. Then there exists a constantσ=σ(n,p,q,s,Λ,α,C1,C2,\sigma=\sigma(n,p,q,s,\Lambda,\alpha,C_{1},C_{2},μ1,μ2,[w]p,L,M,δ)∈(0,1)\mu_{1},\mu_{2},[w]_{p},L,M,\delta)\in(0,1), such that ifw​({Br​(x0):u≤μ1+L})≤σ​w​(Br​(x0)),w(\{B_{r}(x_{0}):u\leq\mu_{1}+L\})\leq\sigma w(B_{r}(x_{0})),

thenu≥μ1+δ​Lu\geq\mu_{1}+\delta LinBr2​(x0)B_{\frac{r}{2}}(x_{0}).

## Proof.

## 4.3.3Weak Harnack inequality

## Proof of Theorem1.6.

We begin with the observation that for0<l≤p00<l\leq p_{0}, Hölder’s inequality together with Lemma4.1yields the estimate (1.10). Thus, it suffices to prove (1.10) forp0<l<κ​(p−1)p_{0}<l<\kappa(p-1). In this regard, our objective is to obtain a reverse Hölder estimate by using Moser’s iteration technique. To this end, we assume12≤τ1≤τ2≤34\frac{1}{2}\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\frac{3}{4},τ~=τ1+τ22\tilde{\tau}=\frac{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}}{2}andφ∈Cc∞​(Bτ~​r​(x0))\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{\tilde{\tau}r}(x_{0}))is a nonnegative function such that0≤φ≤10\leq\varphi\leq 1inBτ~​r​(x0)B_{\tilde{\tau}r}(x_{0}),φ≡1\varphi\equiv 1inBτ1​r​(x0)B_{\tau_{1}r}(x_{0})and|∇φ|≤8(τ2−τ1)​r|\nabla\varphi|\leq\frac{8}{(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})r}inBτ~​r​(x0)B_{\tilde{\tau}r}(x_{0}). Fort>0t>0, we denote byv=(u+t)p−ηpv=(u+t)^{\frac{p-\eta}{p}}. ## Proof of Corollary1.8.

Sinceuuis weak subsolution of equation (1.1),v=−uv=-uis a weak supersolution ofℳα​v=𝒢​(x,v)​in​Ω,\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\,v=\mathcal{G}(x,v)\text{ in }\Omega,

where𝒢​(x,v):=−ℱ​(x,−v)\mathcal{G}(x,v):=-\mathcal{F}(x,-v)with𝒢\mathcal{G}satisfying (1.2). Moreover,vvis bounded below inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}. Hence, by using Theorem1.7, we conclude thatuuis upper semicontinuous. This completes the proof.
∎
